I. 
Introduction 
1 
Q. 
Please identify the members of the Electric Infrastructure and 
2 
Operations Panel. 
3 
A. 
The Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel (“Panel”) consists of 
4 
Matthew Barnett, Wajiha Mahmoud, Bart Franey, Kevin Stablewski, Brian 
5 
Hayduk, and John-Paul Knauss. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Is this the same Panel that previously submitted direct and corrections 
8 
and updates testimony in this proceeding? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  The Panel provided testimony as part of the Company’s July 31, 2020 
10 
and October 14, 2020 filings.  The terms defined in the Panel’s July 31 and 
11 
October 14 testimonies have the same meanings here. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s rebuttal testimony? 
14 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s rebuttal testimony is to respond to issues raised 
15 
in the testimony of the Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) Electric 
16 
Infrastructure and Operations Panel (“SEIOP”) on the Company’s 
17 
infrastructure and operations plans presented in this case.  The Panel also 
18 
responds to the testimonies of the Staff Efficiency and Sustainability Panel 
19 
(“SESP”), the Staff Information Technology Panel (“SITP”), the Staff 
20 


--- Page 4 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 2 of 58 
 
Accounting Panel (“SAP”), and the Staff Electric and Gas Rates Panel 
1 
(“SEGRP”).  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please summarize the Panel’s response to Staff’s testimony. 
4 
A. 
The SEIOP proposes a number of adjustments to the Company’s capital 
5 
plan and O&M programs with which the Company disagrees.  The Panel’s 
6 
rebuttal testimony directly addresses the most significant of the SEIOP’s 
7 
proposed adjustments and explains why such adjustments should be 
8 
rejected.  Where the Company accepts an adjustment or agrees with an issue 
9 
raised by Staff or another party, such acceptance or agreement is 
10 
affirmatively noted.  To the extent the Company does not specifically 
11 
address an adjustment proposed by Staff, such silence should be construed 
12 
as opposition to the proposed adjustment.     
13 
 
14 
Q. 
How is the remainder of the Panel’s testimony structured? 
15 
A. 
In Section II, the Panel addresses the SEIOP’s proposed adjustments to the 
16 
Company’s electric capital infrastructure investment plan.  We also 
17 
address issues related to energy storage projects.  In Section III, the Panel 
18 
addresses the SEIOP’s recommendations on the Company’s proposed 
19 
O&M plans, including storm cost recovery, incremental labor additions, 
20 
the Operational Telecommunications (“OpTel”) Tier 3 Research program, 
21

Page 3 of 58 
 
transportation expense, Streetlight Contractor program, and O&M expense 
1 
related to capital.  Finally, in Section IV, the Panel addresses IT-related 
2 
issues impacting the electric business.   
3

II. 
Capital Investment Plan 
1 
A. 
Impact to Overall Capital Plan and Net Plant in Service 
2 
Q. 
What is the impact of the SEIOP’s recommended adjustments to the 
3 
Panel’s proposed capital plan? 
4 
A. 
The SEIOP’s recommended adjustments to the Company’s proposed 
5 
electric capital plan reduce net plant in service and depreciation expense as 
6 
shown in the table below.  The capital expenditure adjustments also reduce 
7 
the corresponding O&M expenses related to capital.   
8 
SEIOP 
Adjustments 
Rate Year 
Data Year 1 
Data Year 2 
Net Plant in 
Service 
($13.4M) 
($67.5M) 
($160.3M)

($5.27M)

($8.49M) 
 
9 
As discussed in detail below, the Company has presented an investment 
10 
plan that will allow the continued provision of safe and reliable service, 
11 
while at the same time introduce a step change to increased resiliency for 
12 
customers who may be otherwise negatively impacted by volatile weather 
13 
events, and advancement of the State’s ambitious clean energy goals 
14 
ultimately enabling a more dynamic, animated energy marketplace to 
15 


--- Page 8 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 6 of 58 
 
deliver greater benefits to customers.  The SEIOP’s proposed reductions 
1 
would impair the Company’s ability to meet these responsibilities. 
2 
 
3 
Therefore, the Company disagrees with the SEIOP’s adjustments to the 
4 
electric capital plan and the corresponding reductions in net plant, 
5 
depreciation, and O&M expense related to capital.  For Staff’s other 
6 
adjustments impacting electric net plant and depreciation not discussed in 
7 
this testimony, please refer to the rebuttal testimonies of the Company’s 
8 
Revenue Requirements Panel and Company Witness Dr. Kateregga. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please explain why the Company’s proposed resiliency investments are 
11 
necessary and should be approved by the Commission. 
12 
A. 
The Company created a spending rationale specifically for resiliency 
13 
projects in recognition of the growing importance of storm hardening and 
14 
enhancing the system’s ability to recover quickly following severe weather 
15 
events.  This spending rationale includes, among other things, the projects 
16 
in the Transmission Resiliency – Survivability Program, Fault Location 
17 
Isolation and Service Restoration (“FLISR”) Program, and Targeted Feeder 
18 
Tie Enhancement Program that the Panel addresses below.  The increased 
19 
focus on system resiliency presented in this case is warranted given events 
20 
such as Tropical Storm Isaias, which severely impacted numerous utilities 
21 


--- Page 9 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 7 of 58 
 
across New York, resulting in nearly 900,000 peak outages in the 
1 
southeastern region of the State and extended service interruptions.  Such 
2 
events appear to be increasing in frequency, and the SEIOP recognizes the 
3 
rise in storm intensity in recent years (at 26).  At the same time, the State 
4 
and the Commission have set out clear expectations that electric systems 
5 
must be able to better withstand, and recover more quickly from, such 
6 
events.  The SEIOP recommends reducing funding for resiliency projects 
7 
by 46 percent in FY 2022, or $14 million; in total, the SEIOP proposes a 
8 
reduction of 16 percent for resiliency projects between FY22 and FY25, or 
9 
$68 million.  This level of reductions will impede the Company’s ability to 
10 
meet the increased expectations for resiliency shared by the Commission, 
11 
the State, the Company and customers alike. The Company’s proposed 
12 
investments and programs are aligned with these critical priorities, and 
13 
approval of the plan is vital to enabling the Company to deliver on these 
14 
priorities. 
15 
 
16 
B. 
Transmission Capital Plan 
17 
Q. 
What does the SEIOP recommend with respect to the Company’s 
18 
electric transmission capital plan?  
19 
A. 
The SEIOP generally agrees that the Company has justified its transmission 
20 
system investment needs (at 40); however, the SEIOP does not agree with 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 8 of 58 
 
the Company’s proposed investment levels for FY 2022 through FY 2025 
1 
to address those needs.  The SEIOP recommends certain specific downward 
2 
adjustments associated with the Company’s Transmission Resiliency 
3 
Program.  In addition, the SEIOP also recommends a general downward 
4 
adjustment to the balance of the Company’s overall transmission 
5 
investment capital plan proposal based on historical spend and budget 
6 
levels.  Taking the specific and general adjustments together, the SEIOP 
7 
recommends a 19 percent annual average downward adjustment to the 
8 
Company’s proposed transmission capital investment budget for the period 
9 
FY 2022 through FY 2025.  This results in a reduction in annual 
10 
transmission capital expenditures of $30.9 million, $41.8 million, $67.2 
11 
million, and $87.1 million for FY 2022 through FY 2025, respectively.    
12 
 
13 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with the SEIOP’s recommendation for a general 
14 
downward adjustment based on a three-year historical average?   
15 
A.  
No, we do not.  Separate from the specific downward adjustment to the 
16 
Transmission Resiliency Program, the SEIOP recommends (at 48) a general 
17 
reduction to the entire transmission capital plan.  The SEIOP proposes that 
18 
the FY 2022 capital budget be set to the average of the Company’s historical 
19 
FY 2019 and FY 2020 budget and the forecast FY 2021 budget, adjusted 
20 
for an inflation factor of 3.6 percent, and increases the FY 2023, FY 2024, 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 9 of 58 
 
and FY 2025 budgets for inflation.  In addition to recommending future 
1 
investment levels based on average spending for the past three years, the 
2 
SEIOP asserts that its proposed reductions are intended to “encourage 
3 
greater efficiencies and innovations in the Company’s planning, design, 
4 
procurement, project management, and construction of [transmission 
5 
projects].” 
6 
 
7 
The SEIOP’s general downward adjustment using a three-year historical 
8 
average fails to recognize the step change from historic levels of the 
9 
investments necessary to respond to the changes in customer demands and 
10 
New York’s ambitious clean energy goals.  As the Commission stated in its 
11 
May 14, 2020, Order on Transmission Planning Pursuant to the 
12 
Accelerated Renewable Energy Growth and Community Benefit Act in Case 
13 
20-E-0197, “New York has enacted the most aggressive climate policy 
14 
legislation in the country through the Climate Leadership and Community 
15 
Protection Act (CLCPA),” and “achieving the CLCPA climate protection 
16 
targets requires restructuring and repurposing the State’s electric 
17 
transmission and distribution infrastructure . . . .”  The Company’s proposed 
18 
transmission capital budget presents the investment levels required not only 
19 
to meet the Company’s obligations to continue to provide safe and reliable 
20 
service, but also to fund new, cost-effective initiatives such as Multi-Value 
21 


--- Page 12 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 10 of 58 
 
Transmission (“MVT”) projects, the costs of which would not be reflected 
1 
in the Company’s historical capital expenditures.  The SEIOP’s 
2 
methodology also does not take into account the need for incremental 
3 
capital expenditure for mandated transmission projects that support regional 
4 
transmission service – namely the Western New York and Energy Highway 
5 
Public Policy Transmission projects.  As with MVT, these investments are 
6 
incremental and are not represented in a three-year historical analysis.  
7 
Additionally, these Public Policy Transmission projects are specifically 
8 
supported by Commission orders, and the Company must continue to 
9 
construct them.  Finally, the SEIOP’s adjustment does not recognize the 
10 
incremental increase in investment for OpTel assets that are fundamentally 
11 
required to continue system operations and replace current services being 
12 
retired.  Failure to account for such incremental capital expenditures will 
13 
severely challenge the Company’s ability to deliver the transmission 
14 
investments identified in the plan and will require the Company to make 
15 
difficult prioritization decisions, which will inevitably lead to elimination 
16 
or deferral of necessary projects, potentially including asset condition 
17 
projects, OpTel projects, and some or all MVT projects.  
18 
 
19 


--- Page 13 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 11 of 58 
 
Q. 
What are the new programs that support current and future system 
1 
needs that may need to be deferred under the SEIOP’s recommended 
2 
transmission capital expenditure reductions?   
3 
A.  
The magnitude of transmission capital expenditure reductions proposed by 
4 
the SEIOP exceed $225 million in total over the period FY 2022 through 
5 
FY 2025; the capital expenditure reduction is in excess of 25 percent for FY 
6 
2025 in particular.  As discussed above, the general reduction method 
7 
employed by the SEIOP fails to acknowledge several categories of spend 
8 
and drivers that were not reflected in historical costs and which therefore 
9 
should not be reduced based on a historical baseline. The following project 
10 
types were not reflected in historical costs, putting their delivery at risk if 
11 
not funded appropriately:  
12 
• MVT projects are intended to address asset condition needs that are 
13 
not an urgent risk to safe and reliable service, and at the same time, 
14 
provide benefit to customers by increasing deliverability of 
15 
renewable energy resources as directed by the CLCPA and the 
16 
Accelerated Renewable Energy Growth and Community Benefit 
17 
Act.  With the SEIOP’s recommended adjustments, funds targeted 
18 
for MVT projects will likely need to be reallocated to higher priority 
19 
safety and reliability projects and deferred until asset condition 
20 
needs on these circuits become a priority. 
21 


--- Page 14 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 12 of 58 
 
• OpTel projects to replace obsolete analog circuits and transition 
1 
legacy leased line services to private telecommunication 
2 
infrastructure.  The deliverability of these incremental projects 
3 
would be at risk if the SEIOP’s recommendation is adopted.  Local 
4 
telecommunications providers are terminating analog services and 
5 
contracts while pursuing copper to fiber conversions.  Several 
6 
Company substation protection functions are currently utilizing 
7 
these services, making it essential that the Company transition to 
8 
alternative means of communication, such as private, dedicated 
9 
circuits that provide the required latency, bandwidth, and quality of 
10 
service necessary for the reliability and security of the electric grid.  
11 
Because this work is incremental to historical expenditures, without 
12 
specifically and incrementally accounting for the cost associated 
13 
with leased line migration and multiplexer (“DMX”) replacement, 
14 
the Company may need to defer other asset condition projects that 
15 
are critical to continued safe and reliable system operations.     
16 
• The Company is required to construct several Public Policy 
17 
Transmission projects under Commission orders.  As stated above, 
18 
this work is incremental to the historical expenditures considered by 
19 
the SEIOP in developing its general reduction recommendation.  
20 
Without specifically and incrementally accounting for the increased 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 13 of 58 
 
capital expenditures for these projects, the Company will need to 
1 
defer asset condition projects that are critical to continued safe and 
2 
reliable system operations.   
3 
• The Worst Performing Circuit Program is a new quantitative 
4 
program that prioritizes mitigation of chronically poor-performing 
5 
circuits that adversely impact service and power quality.  The 
6 
number of circuits that can be addressed annually under this 
7 
program will be reduced. 
8 
In summary, the impact of such capital expenditure adjustments for 
9 
transmission, as recommended by the SEIOP, will require the Company to 
10 
defer a number of projects and initiatives that directly support customer 
11 
reliability and investments needed to enable the State’s clean energy goals. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with SEIOP’s downward adjustments for the 
14 
Company’s Transmission Resiliency – Survivability Program? 
15 
A. 
No, we do not.  The SEIOP (at 46) proposes downward adjustments of 
16 
$0.200 million, $1.441 million, $6.337 million, and $3.124 million in FY 
17 
2022, FY 2023, FY 2024, and FY 2025, respectively, based on its 
18 
recommendation that the Company postpone the following resiliency 
19 
projects:  
20 


--- Page 16 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 14 of 58 
 
• Clay-Dewitt Resiliency Project – Project would eliminate the 
1 
sustained outage to 9,500 customers and a momentary outage to 
2 
1,500 customers for the loss of the Clay – Dewitt line.  
3 
• Dewitt-Tilden Resiliency Project – Project would eliminate the 
4 
sustained outage of 12,000 customers for the loss of the Dewitt – 
5 
Tilden line.   
6 
• New Krumkill Resiliency Project – Project would eliminate the 
7 
sustained outage of 6,800 customers at New Krumkill due to the loss 
8 
of a line between Albany and Mckownville. 
9 
• North Troy-Hoosick Resiliency Project – Project would enable 
10 
quicker restoration of 5,300 customers from the loss of the North 
11 
Troy – Hoosick line.  
12 
• Rotterdam-Maplewood Resiliency Project – An outage of the first 
13 
line out of Rotterdam and the first line out of Maplewood will result 
14 
in an interruption to 42,000 customers.  Other combinations of line 
15 
outages would also interrupt a significant number of customers.  
16 
Through constructing a new station at Riverview Road, a third 
17 
source will be added into this load pocket, and the number of 
18 
customers interrupted for an outage of any two lines would be 
19 
reduced from up to 42,000 to no more than 16,700.  
20 


--- Page 17 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 15 of 58 
 
• S Owego-LHH Resiliency Project – Project would enable much 
1 
quicker restoration of 6,600 customers from the loss of the Indeck–
2 
Lighthouse Hill line. 
3 
The SEIOP states that historical outage performance of these circuits does 
4 
not demonstrate an immediate need for such work.  However, as provided 
5 
in the Company’s responses to Information Requests (“IRs”) DPS-491, 
6 
492, 498, 499, 500, and 502, the justification for the projects listed above, 
7 
as for all projects under this spending rationale, is to address low frequency-
8 
high impact events proactively.  For example, a substation built in a 100-
9 
year floodplain may have never experienced any flooding, but if it were to 
10 
become flooded, it would create a large-scale, long-duration outage for 
11 
customers served by that substation.  Proactive investments such as the 
12 
Company proposes are key to enabling a more resilient system and 
13 
providing continued reliability in the face of more severe and volatile 
14 
weather events.   
15 
 
16 
As stated previously, extreme weather events appear to be increasing in 
17 
frequency and intensity, and their disruptive impacts on customers and 
18 
communities can be significant.  To identify and prioritize system risks to 
19 
low-frequency, high-impact events, the Company performed a deterministic 
20 
review of the number of customers that could be affected by a failure of a 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 16 of 58 
 
transmission element.  These incremental investments are specifically 
1 
intended to provide the step change in resiliency to volatile weather events 
2 
and fulfill expectations of the State, the Commission, and customers.  Given 
3 
the cost impact relative to the potential customer protection from sustained 
4 
outages, the Company maintains that the funding and timing for its 
5 
Transmission Resiliency Program remain as originally proposed.   
6 
 
7 
C. 
Sub-Transmission Capital Plan 
8 
Q. 
What adjustments does the SEIOP propose regarding the Company’s 
9 
Sub-Transmission capital plan? 
10 
A. 
The SEIOP recommends certain downward adjustments associated with the 
11 
Company’s Resiliency Sub-Transmission Automation Program.  In 
12 
addition, the SEIOP also recommends general downward adjustments to the 
13 
Company’s overall sub-transmission investment plan for FY 2022.  Taking 
14 
the specific and general adjustments together, the SEIOP recommends an 
15 
18 percent annual average downward adjustment to the Company’s 
16 
proposed sub-transmission capital investment budget for the period FY 
17 
2022 – FY 2025.  This results in a reduction in annual transmission capital 
18 
expenditures of $7.3 million, $18.3 million, $15.1 million, and $0.8 million 
19 
for FY 2022 through FY 2025, respectively. 
20 
 
 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 17 of 58 
 
Q. 
The SEIOP recommends (at 58) a general downward adjustment of 15 
1 
percent to the Company’s proposed sub-transmission capital budget.  
2 
Does the Company agree with this adjustment? 
3 
A. 
No.  Similar to the proposed adjustment to the transmission capital plan, the 
4 
SEIOP recommends a general downward adjustment to the sub-
5 
transmission capital plan based on the view that future investment in the 
6 
sub-transmission system should be based on the Company’s recent 
7 
historical expenditures, and that reducing the funding allowance to a level 
8 
substantially below the Company’s projected level of spend to deliver the 
9 
planned investments will encourage cost efficiencies.  The SEIOP uses a 
10 
two-year average of FY 2020 actual spending and FY 2021 budgeted levels, 
11 
plus inflation, to establish a revised FY 2022 budget.   
12 
 
13 
The Company disagrees with the SEIOP’s proposed adjustments. The 
14 
Company’s sub-transmission system continues to face an increasing level 
15 
of asset condition needs.  In fact, asset condition needs and damage/failure 
16 
account for approximately 85 percent of the FY 2022 capital expenditure 
17 
forecast, and 90 percent of the total projected capital expenditures in FY 
18 
2022 through FY 2025.  The Company’s emphasis on resiliency focuses on 
19 
mitigating risk associated with asset condition and reliability challenges on 
20 
the sub-transmission system.  This is particularly true for areas of the system 
21 


--- Page 20 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 18 of 58 
 
subject to increased levels of outage risk due to the nature of the local 
1 
system (i.e., radial lines in densely forested areas) or system age or 
2 
condition.  By limiting the Company to funding levels on par with historical 
3 
levels, the Company will have limited options to improve resiliency for 
4

The proposed projects under the Substation Indoor program will replace 
1

3

5

17

18

under this program.  Based on the foregoing, the Commission should

4

5

6

7

8

9 
Handbook’s suggestion that service value (or rather, value of avoided 
10 
service interruption) “should be determined based on the customers’ 
11 
willingness to pay for reliability.”  Additionally, this method of estimating 
12 
the value of service has been utilized in justifying other investments 
13 
proposed by the Company and other New York utilities that were approved 
14 
by the Commission and/or supported by Staff.  The Company understands 
15 
and agrees with the need to ensure investments are cost effective and 
16 
believes it has achieved this goal.  More importantly, however, this 
17 
investment is directly targeted at the fundamental need for a step change in 
18 
resilience supported and demanded by the State, the Commission and our 
19 
customers as spoken to throughout this testimony.  FLISR addresses this 
20 
immediate need from both a reliability and resiliency perspective.  For 
21

Incremental O&M 
8 
A. 
OpTel Tier 3 Research Study 
9 
Q. 
Why does the SEIOP recommend (at 95) a downward adjustment of 
10 
the full requested amount of $2.25 million in the Rate Year and $0.75 
11 
million in Data Year 1 associated with OpTel Tier 3 Research?   
12 
A. 
The SEIOP argues that given the existing efforts and analysis already 
13 
underway by the Company and the West Monroe Partners (“WMP”) 
14 
roadmap for the Operational Telecommunications Modernization Strategy, 
15 
there is not a need for an additional study to be performed in this area.  The 
16 
SEIOP feels the Company should use its existing experience and the results 
17 
of the WMP roadmap to determine any additional next steps and associated 
18 
communications solutions. 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 35 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 33 of 58 
 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with the SEIOP’s recommended adjustment to 
1 
the OpTel Tier 3 Research program?  
2 
A.       No, we do not.  The OpTel Tier 3 research program is the natural and 
3 
intended next step following the WMP strategy paper and will provide 
4 
valuable information that is not available through existing resources.  The 
5 
strategy was to select from a wide range of possible wireless technologies 
6 
that can support various use cases applicable to today’s environment and 
7 
expected future growth opportunities, to arrive at a narrowed list of 
8 
technologies for which the Company must perform in depth evaluation and 
9 
feasibility analysis prior to final selection and implementation.  The 
10 
Company has performed preliminary evaluation of several frequency bands 
11 
(as part of the WMP strategy development efforts) and has shortlisted 
12 
preferred solutions as either 700 MHz or 900 MHz licensed technologies, 
13 
primarily due to their favorable far-reaching propagation, which in-turn 
14 
greatly reduces the required site count and the associated cost of the 
15 
network build and operation.   
16 
 
17 
The OpTel Tier 3 Research program will provide funding to perform testing 
18 
in a controlled lab environment beginning in 2021 to further evaluate and 
19 
understand the down-selected technology options and spectrum against test 
20 
cases.  The program will also provide funding to develop a more detailed 
21 


--- Page 36 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 34 of 58 
 
implementation plan, including detailed scopes and estimates, based on the 
1 
selected technology.  
2 
 
3 
In sum, the OpTel Tier 3 Research program is a detailed technology 
4 
evaluation that will provide the foundation for additional plans to scale such 
5 
solutions for system-wide rollout across the Company’s service territory.  
6 
Therefore, the Commission should approve the proposed expenditure. 
7

the minimum threshold of incremental costs of an individual major storm 
5 
must be greater than or equal to $0.400 million for each qualifying region, 
6 
unless the sum of all major storm events costing less than $0.400 million 
7 
reaches $0.800 million, in which case there is no cost threshold (for the 
8 
remainder of the year).  The SEIOP proposes increasing this threshold to 
9 
$0.750 million to be applied to all qualifying regions combined in the 
10 
Company’s service territory for a given storm event and eliminating the 
11 
cumulative cost exception. 
12 
 
13 
The Company disagrees with the SEIOP’s recommendation.  The major 
14 
storm deferral criteria is designed to ensure that storms are of sufficient 
15 
magnitude to justify deferral recovery.  The threshold of $0.400 million per 
16 
storm is intended to exclude storms from deferral that meet the reliability 
17 
threshold but do not represent a financial burden high enough to be 
18 
considered outside of the scope of minor storm funding.  The second 
19 
element of the cost threshold (deferral when the sum of otherwise major 
20 
storms exceeds $0.800 million in one year) is designed to allow deferral 
21 


--- Page 39 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 37 of 58 
 
recovery for the aggregate cost of multiple major storms events that do not 
1 
exceed the threshold.  As discussed in the Panel’s direct testimony, the 
2 
Company expects (even without the SEIOP’s proposed cost threshold 
3 
change) that minor storm events, as well as the costs to restore customers 
4 
from those increased events, will continue to increase in volume and 
5 
unpredictability in the coming years, primarily driven by increases of 
6 
intense weather patterns and volatility.  The SEIOP acknowledged this 
7 
trend, stating in its testimony (at 26) that “there has been a rise in storm 
8 
intensity in recent years.”  Additionally, given the Company’s commitment 
9 
to investing in reliability and resiliency, and the State’s focus on storm 
10 
response, it is the Company’s expectation that an increasing number of 
11 
storms of similar intensity to those historically experienced should be 
12 
mitigated with shorter restoration durations, further burdening the minor 
13 
storms funding.  The Company believes the major storm deferral criteria 
14 
cost threshold should remain as is to avoid adding excessive and 
15 
inappropriate costs to the minor storms funding. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SEIOP’s calculation of the major storm 
18 
expense reserve? 
19 
A.  
No.  Based on its proposed modifications to the major storm deferral 
20 
threshold, the SEIOP reduces the Company’s major storm expense reserve 
21

from $31 million to $30 million.  The Company believes the cost threshold 
1 
for major storm cost deferral should not be modified, and that the 
2

3

5 
2. 
Minor Storms 
6 
Q. 
The SEIOP recommends (at 136) setting the Rate Year minor storm 
7 
allowance using a three-year historical average.  Does the Panel agree? 
8 
A. 
No, we do not.  The Company’s proposed minor storm allowance is based 
9 
on the actual expense incurred in the Historic Test Year, adjusted for 
10 
inflation.  The SEIOP’s calculation of the minor storm expense using a 
11 
three-year historical average, adjusted for the SEIOP’s proposed new major 
12 
storm deferral threshold, results in a downward adjustment of $5.383 
13 
million in the Rate Year.  As discussed in the Panel’s direct testimony, 
14 
minor storm costs have been increasing year-over-year, and the Company 
15 
believes this trend will continue.  The Company has been consistently 
16 
working to mitigate the potentially severe impact to customers from 
17 
increasing severe weather events and will continue to improve through 
18 
further system hardening, new and growing programs to improve resiliency 
19 
and preparing aggressively for forecasted events with pre-staged crews.  
20 
The positive benefits for customers will be shorter duration events which 
21 


--- Page 41 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 39 of 58 
 
will then also drive more minor storm costs that would otherwise have 
1 
qualified for deferral as a major event.  Given the continuing upward trend 
2 
in storm costs, a forecast using three years of historical costs will be less 
3 
accurate than one that uses information from the most recent year.  
4 
Therefore, the Company believes that using the actual minor storm costs 
5 
incurred in the Historic Test Year will provide a better basis than the 
6 
SEIOP’s use of a three-year historical average. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What support does the Panel have that the Historic Test Year is a better 
9 
indicator of future minor storm costs than a three-year historical 
10 
average? 
11 
A. 
Using actual minor storm costs since 2013, the Company analyzed the 
12 
historical accuracy of both the SEIOP’s and the Company’s methodologies 
13 
to establish the Rate Year minor storm expense.  Applying the SEIOP’s 
14 
methodology to 2017-2019 actual costs, as shown in Table 2 below, the 
15 
three-year historical average costs, adjusted for inflation, would produce 
16 
funding levels that are far below the actual incurred costs:  $11.43 million 
17 
versus actual costs of $17.35 million in 2017 (52 percent underfunding), 
18 
$12.14 million versus actual costs of $25.64 million in 2018 (111 percent 
19 
underfunding), and $14.43 million versus actual costs of $31.22 million in 
20

Page 40 of 58 
 
2019 (116 percent underfunding).  This is an average underfunding error of 
1 
95 percent, representing over $36 million of underestimate inaccuracy.   
2 
 
3 
 
Applying the Company’s calculation to that same timeframe shows the 
4 
actual incurred costs would still be more than the Company’s estimate, but 
5 
by lesser amounts.  Using the Company’s Historic Test Year approach 
6 
would lead to the following annual funding amounts for 2017-2019: $11.24 
7 
million in 2017 (54 percent underfunding), $13.34 million in 2018 (92 
8 
percent underfunding), and $18.04 million in 2019 (73 percent 
9

10 
representing approximately $31.6 million of underestimate inaccuracy.

Q. 
Did the Panel identify any other issues with the SEIOP’s calculation of 
9 
the minor storm reserve? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  If the Commission were to accept the SEIOP’s recommendation to 
11 
apply a $0.750 million threshold to each major storm event to determine 
12 
deferrable costs, the SEIOP’s adjustment to the Rate Year minor storm 
13 
allowance to include major storm costs that do not exceed this threshold 
14 
(i.e., major non-deferrable storm costs) is incorrect. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
For clarity, please explain why an adjustment to minor storm expense 
17 
allowance is necessary related to the proposed threshold by the SEIOP. 
18 


--- Page 44 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 42 of 58 
 
A. 
Storm events not meeting the criteria of a major storm as defined in 16 
1 
NYCRR § 97.1(c) are minor storms.  Minor storm costs are recovered 
2 
through an expense allowance in base rates. Storm events qualifying as 
3 
major can be eligible for deferral treatment and recovered through the 
4 
reconciling storm fund.  Major storm costs that do not qualify for deferral 
5 
treatment, for example costs for a storm that does not exceed the per-storm 
6 
threshold, are the equivalent of minor storm costs and recovered through 
7 
the minor storm expense allowance.  Therefore, determination of a minor 
8 
storm expense allowance must include minor storm costs and major non-
9 
deferrable storm costs. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please explain why the SEIOP’s minor storm expense allowance is 
12 
incorrect. 
13 
A. 
The SEIOP’s adjustment to minor storm expense is calculated considering 
14 
the major storm events with incremental costs above the $0.400 million per 
15 
storm threshold pursuant to the Company’s current rate plan, and that are at 
16 
or below the proposed $0.750 million per storm threshold.  In other words, 
17 
the SEIOP’s methodology ignores major storms with costs below the 
18 
$0.400 million threshold, as if those costs are already reflected in minor 
19 
storm costs, which they are not. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 45 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 43 of 58 
 
Q. 
What does the SEIOP use as the basis for a minor storm expense 
1 
allowance before applying its adjustments? 
2 
A. 
The SEIOP uses the minor storm costs provided in Attachment 2 to the 
3 
Company’s response to IR DPS-525 as its basis for a minor storm expense 
4 
allowance before applying its adjustments. 
5 
 
6

7

A. 
No.  Minor storm costs provided in Attachment 2 to the response to IR DPS-
9 
525 do not include major non-deferrable storm costs that result from the 
10 
application of the $0.400 million threshold.  Furthermore, the Company 
11 
normalized out all major deferrable and major non-deferrable storm costs 
12 
from the Historic Test Year as shown on Exhibit __ (RRP-11CU), 
13 
Workpapers to Exhibit RRP-3CU, Schedule 2, Workpaper 2 and 
14 
Workpaper 3.  Therefore, to determine the proper minor storm expense 
15 
allowance, the SEIOP’s adjustment to minor storm costs should include all 
16 
incremental costs for any major storm event for which the total incremental 
17 
costs do not exceed the $0.750 million threshold.  The SEIOP’s adjustment 
18 
does not.

15 
Q.  
The SEIOP recommends (at 135) rejecting the proposed minor storm 
16 
cost reconciliation mechanism.  Does the Company agree? 
17 
A.  
No.  The SEIOP does not support the Company’s proposal to place a two-
18 
way tracker with a +/- $10 million deadband on the minor storms fund.  In 
19 
justifying this position, the SEIOP states that, “[m]inor storms are a 
20 
recurring cost that should be forecastable with reasonable accuracy. 
21 


--- Page 48 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 46 of 58 
 
Therefore, a deadband is not necessary.”  The Company disagrees with this 
1 
rationale and believes the modification to the minor storm fund is justified 
2 
and necessary to mitigate risk associated with increasingly volatile weather 
3 
patterns and associated storm restoration costs.  As shown above, 
4 
determining future costs based upon historical levels alone has not proven 
5 
to be an accurate methodology.  Although the Company could have 
6 
proposed a different methodology to forecast future costs, potentially 
7 
through utilizing regression analysis or other statistical means, this would 
8 
not offer the same level of customer protection as the Company’s proposal.  
9 
The Company reasonably expects minor storm costs to grow due to 
10 
continued weather volatility; there will also be additional pressure on minor 
11 
storm costs given the enhanced focus on effectively managing the severity 
12 
and duration of impact to customers of this volatile weather and thereby 
13 
reducing the chances of some significant events from becoming deferrable 
14 
major storms.  It is not possible, however, to predict which storms will be 
15 
impacted or precisely how.  These shifting and sometimes competing 
16 
factors will unavoidably lead to variation between any forecast and the 
17 
actual results, and as history shows, those variances can be extreme.  If the 
18 
Company were to update its forecast of future costs based upon statistically 
19 
accepted methods, and subsequently minor storm costs did not increase as 
20 
reasonably expected, then actual costs would be less than forecast, and 
21 


--- Page 49 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 47 of 58 
 
customers would have ultimately overpaid for services not required.  In the 
1 
more likely alternative, where actual costs continue to rise at a level even 
2 
beyond forecast, the Company is expected to bear the cost difference 
3 
without recovery.  This would ultimately lead to an inefficient reallocation 
4 
of funds intended to support other functions, negatively impacting the 
5 
Company’s ability to deliver on all the expectations of New York 
6 
customers, which are continually increasing to include improved resiliency 
7 
and delivery of the clean energy transition.  For these reasons, the deadband 
8 
and reconciliation should be accepted as proposed by the Company.

1 
B. 
Grid Modernization Initiatives 
2 
Q.  
Why does the SEIOP recommend (at 146) removing the full costs 
3 
associated with the INOC initiative?  Does the Panel agree with the 
4 
recommendation? 
5 
A. 
The SEIOP recommends removing all costs associated with the Integrated 
6 
Network Operations Center (“INOC”) project because the Company did not 
7 
provide sufficient support for how the budget was developed.  The 
8 
Company disagrees with the SEIOP’s position and submits that the 
9 
Company has provided sufficient justification for the project and its 
10 
benefits.  Existing manual practices cannot keep pace with the increasing 
11 
volume and flexibility required for grid modernization. Therefore, the 
12 
Company plans to implement a Telecommunications Operations 
13 
Management System (“TOMS”) tool for the planning, engineering, 
14 
commissioning, management and operations of the Company’s OpTel 
15 
systems that integrates with a planned INOC to monitor and manage the 
16 
grid network.  This provides end-to-end automation of network operations 
17 
essential to meet growing network demands.  
18 
 
19 
Once the current state of the existing network is captured and maintained 
20 
within TOMS, the performance of the network will be managed by the 
21 


--- Page 55 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 53 of 58 
 
INOC.  The INOC will actively monitor, manage, and maintain the 
1 
integrated set of communications-based services and infrastructure in a 
2 
similar fashion as a System Control Center manages the power grid.  As 
3 
with the carrier-grade network operation centers of traditional telecom 
4 
service providers, the INOC will provide real-time insight and control of 
5 
network performance through a “single pane of glass” down to the lowest 
6 
telecommunications asset (i.e., grid edge equipment).  Considering that 
7 
there are many private wireline and wireless networks deployed throughout 
8 
the Company using varying technologies, the INOC brings together all 
9 
disparate network management systems and/or management tools into a 
10 
single centralized platform to maintain a high level of network performance 
11 
across all National Grid telecommunications devices.  
12 
 
13 
The INOC will provide active monitoring of both IT and OT related assets 
14 
and infrastructure, ensuring a healthy heartbeat for all business 
15 
operations.  This INOC will be fully capable of integrating a vast array 
16 
of different protocols, vendors, and service providers, including both public 
17 
and private resources to be monitored, and ensure proper situational 
18 
awareness all the way to the edge consumer as one logical end-to-end 
19 
network.  The INOC will also enable significant improvement in service 
20 
outage restoration and accommodate more proactive response to increasing 
21 


--- Page 56 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 54 of 58 
 
customer demands.  The INOC is a foundational element required to 
1 
become a Distributed System Operator and will facilitate growth for 
2 
emerging on-demand services.  As we transform legacy networks to modern 
3 
technologies, the Company needs to build more robust network architecture 
4 
in a unified and cohesive fashion for sustainability, and careful integration 
5 
of efforts such as the DMX replacement and TOMS implementation are key 
6 
considerations.  The INOC is required to adequately manage and operate 
7 
this transformational network.  
8 
 
9 
The Company is currently running multiple monitoring platforms across 
10 
different network architecture domains leading to fragmented issue or 
11 
incident resolution and network performance monitoring.  These disparate 
12 
monitoring solutions do not provide the real-time integrated information to 
13 
operators that is needed to take timely corrective action.  To maximize 
14 
operational benefit, it is critical to migrate to a centralized and integrated 
15 
network operations center, which can provide real value to the growing 
16 
needs of IT and OT systems.  Please see Exhibit __ (EIOP-4R) for further 
17 
detail on the project implementation timeline, costs, and plan.  The list 
18 
below provides a highlight of the benefits the investment will enable, which 
19 
justify the INOC investment. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 57 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 55 of 58 
 
Benefit Summary: 
1 
• 
Reduce service interruptions and outages by isolating faults and 
2 
dispatching directly to the root cause of the issue.  
3 
• 
Improve service outage restoration by coordinating effectively with 
4 
correct support teams.  
5 
• 
Drive advances in network management center that include automation 
6 
and orchestration.  
7 
• 
Implement centralized network monitoring with ITIL-based service 
8 
management solution.   
9 
• 
Provide end-to-end visibility and monitoring of all network operations 
10 
and SLA-driven network incident resolution, reducing the premium 
11 
being paid to the third-party carriers.  
12 
• 
Accommodate more proactive response to meet existing and increasing 
13 
customer demands.  
14 
• 
Provide a comprehensive and integrated view of all IT and OT services.  
15 
 
16 
Q.  
Does the Panel support the SEIOP’s recommended removal of the full 
17 
costs for the Distribution PI / PI Vision Extension Projects (at 143)? 
18 
A.  
No, we do not.  The Panel disagrees with the SEIOP’s recommendation to 
19 
remove the full costs associated with the Distribution PI & PI Vision 
20 
extension projects.  Separating out a stand-alone distribution PI data 
21 


--- Page 58 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 56 of 58 
 
platform is important to support the evolving nature of the energy delivery 
1 
system and the growth of the Company’s advanced infrastructure, and it 
2 
will deliver benefits such as lower security risks and access to additional 
3 
data on this platform to support advanced load forecasting analytics.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Does the Company support the SITP’s proposed funding decreases for 
6 
Grid Modernization IT projects intended to support the electric 
7 
business? 
8 
A. 
No. As stated previously, the Company’s proposed IT investments, 
9 
including many of the Grid Modernization IT projects, directly support the 
10 
incremental projects proposed by this Panel.  These include ADMS and 
11 
ADMS Mobile Dispatch, TOMS, DMX, the Data Management Platform, 
12 
and GIS Phase II.   
13 
 
14 
TOMS and DMX: TOMS and DMX are essential IT investments (along 
15 
with INOC) to supporting the OpTel initiative.  The DMX IT and 
16 
infrastructure investments are inseparable, and both are required to replace 
17 
antiquated communications equipment.  TOMS is required for efficient 
18 
management of the exponentially growing volume of telecommunications 
19 
infrastructure, as supported by the OpTel Modernization strategy.  
20 
 
21 


--- Page 59 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 57 of 58 
 
ADMS: ADMS phase 1 is a mature in-flight program, so any reduction to 
1 
ADMS phase 1 will risk the in-service date, project scope, and electric 
2 
business benefits.  ADMS phase 2 forecasts are informed by phase 1, and is 
3 
carefully scoped to align with other investments such as AMI to develop 
4 
business benefits.  Reductions to the allowance for ADMS phase 2 will 
5 
create adjustments to the timeline.  The ADMS investment supports core 
6 
business transformation capabilities and integration of distributed energy 
7 
resources supporting State and Company goals.   
8 
 
9 
Data Management Platform: Inadequate funding for the Data Management 
10 
Platform puts other needs at risk including ADMS, planning tool needs and 
11 
DER System Data Portal enhancements.  This investment is necessary to 
12 
enable the right data flows, quality management and governance required 
13 
for operating and planning two-way power flows.  This platform will work 
14 
closely with systems and databases such as GIS, though some of these 
15 
systems also require overhaul or replacement.  
16 
 
17 
GIS: If the Company cannot start work to address its aged system (deployed 
18 
in 2011) the Company will continue to experience performance issues and 
19 
introduce additional risk of system failure.  GIS is the foundation to 
20 
understand the Company’s asset and network configurations that are 
21 


--- Page 60 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
 
 
 
Page 58 of 58 
 
subsequently used by other processes (i.e., OMS/ADMS/Load Flow 
1 
Planning).  A major, long duration system interruption puts those other 
2 
processes at risk as well.    
3 
 
4 
V. 
Conclusion 
5 
Q. 
Does that conclude the Panel’s rebuttal testimony? 
6 
A. 
Yes, it does. 
7

SUBJECT:   Incremental Labor 
Request: 
Note: In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be 
construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in 
original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
For each incremental position/role identified in Exhibit__(EIOP-13), provide the following 
information: 
1.
Identify as a new or existing position/role.
2.
For each new position/role, provide a detailed description/explanation for why the
Company deems this position/role necessary as of the proposed hiring date.
3.
For each existing position/role, provide five years of historical employee levels.
a.
For each historical year, break numbers down by the following and transfer the
annual cumulative number of employees to the next year, accordingly:
i.
Number of employees at start of the year.
ii.
Number of employees added throughout the year.
iii.
Number of employees lost throughout the year.
Response:  
1.
All positions are new.  None of the requested positions are for existing vacancies.
2.
See Attachment 1 for a detailed description of each role and why each position/role is
necessary as of the proposed hiring date.

Role from EIOP-13
Detailed Role Breakdown 
Justification of Role
Distribution Line Mechanic
Distribution Line Mechanic
These incremental internal field positions are proposed by the Company to support implementation of its increased capital investment plan. The 
increased pressure in the contractor market to procure electric field workers continues to create availability challenges and drive price increases in the 
market. 
Transmission Line Worker
Transmission Line Worker
Transmission Line department to assist with meeting the Capital Plan. It takes into consideration the increased capital plan which has nearly a 10% increase 
year over year from FY21 to FY25 for a total increase of approximately 35%. The Transmission Line workers hiring in FY21 and FY23, also takes into 
consideration the progression/ training process of 5 years to get them to a fully functioning Transmission line worker hotstick when hired internally or 
externally at the entry level position of Helper.
Transmission Line Equipment 
Operator
Transmission Line Equipment 
Operator
Transmission Line department to assist with meeting the Capital Plan it also takes into consideration the required development of the additional line workers 
and operators ranging from 2 to 5 years to be fully competent and successfully manage the increased capital plan which has nearly a 10% increase year over 
year from FY21 to FY25 for a total increase of approximately 35% for Transmission Line work
Distribution Designer
Distribution Designer
In conjunction with the increased capital plan for FY 2022 the below positions are a necessity for National Grid Design.  The Design Rep and Drafting Tech 
positions will provide the appropriate internal labor increase to match the increased capital plan.
On average year over year the increased capital spend will be approximately 9%, Design is requesting a onetime increase in labor of 7% to perform the 
additional capital design work.  The work that is not able to be performed by internal labor will be given to design contractors.
PTO Technician
PTO Technician
The need for technical resources in the Protection and Telecommunications specialty continues to grow to meet the increasing demands of a modern grid.  This 
need is best highlighted by the increasing demands for real-time system information and the two-way flow of energy produced by distributed resources. Also, 
this takes into consideration the steady increase in the capital plan from FY21 to FY25. 
Supervisor Design
Supervisor Design
The Design supervisor position is a direct correlation to the increased Design Rep positions along with anticipated external (MSA) design work that will also be 
required to maintain the capital growth plan.
On average year over year the increased capital spend will be approximately 9%, Design is requesting a onetime increase in labor of 7% to perform the 
additional capital design work.  The work that is not able to be performed by internal labor will be given to design contractors.
Supervisor PTO
Supervisor PTO
The need for technical resources in the Protection and Telecommunications specialty continues to grow to meet the increasing demands of a modern grid.  This 
need is best highlighted by the increasing demands for real-time system information and the two-way flow of energy produced by distributed resources. Also, 
this takes into consideration the steady increase in the capital plan from FY21 to FY25. 
Associate Supervisor 
The staffing will reduce the dependence on contracted supervisors for the increase in capital work (who are also in high demand and can leave at any time, 
difficult to retain with the money being offered in the industry and retirees have limited work capacity at 900 hrs. per year)) and also  allow us to staff the 
Article VII projects. These management employees also play a key role in overseeing contractors during storm restoration efforts. 
Senior Supervisor Internal and 
External Capital Delivery 
Supervision
The staffing will reduce the dependence on contracted supervisors for the increase in capital work (who are also in high demand and can leave at any time, 
difficult to retain with the money being offered in the industry and retirees have limited work capacity at 900 hrs. per year)) and also allow us to staff the 
Article VII projects. These management employees also play a key role in overseeing contractors during storm restoration efforts. Additional positions are 
required to meet 
the increase of capital work in Sub-T and Distribution line work of approximately 9% year over year from FY21 to FY25 for a total increase of nearly 30%.
Supervisor, Distribtuion Line 
These incremental internal field positions are proposed by the Company to support implementation of its increased capital investment plan. The trend of 
increased pressure in the contractor market to procure field workers continues to create availability challenges and drive price increases in the market. 
Incremental Full-time Equivalents - Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation
d/b/a National Grid

Role from EIOP-13
Detailed Role Breakdown 
Justification of Role
Incremental Full-time Equivalents - Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel
Supervisor, Distribution Line 
Contracting
The staffing will reduce the dependence on contracted supervisors for the increase in capital work (who are also in high demand and can leave at any time, 
difficult to retain with the money being offered in the industry and retirees have limited work capacity at 900 hrs. per year)) and also allow us to staff the Article 
VII projects. These management employees also play a key role in overseeing contractors during storm restoration efforts. Additional positions are required to 
meet 
the increase of capital work in Sub-T and Distribution line work of approximately 9% year over year from FY21 to FY25 for a total increase of nearly 30%.
Supervisor, Distribution Line 
Services
The staffing will reduce the dependence on contracted supervisors for the increase in capital work (who are also in high demand and can leave at any time, 
difficult to retain with the money being offered in the industry and retirees have limited work capacity at 900 hrs. per year)) and also allow us to staff the Article 
VII projects. These management employees also play a key role in overseeing contractors during storm restoration efforts. Additional positions are required to 
meet 
the increase of capital work in Sub-T and Distribution line work of approximately 9% year over year from FY21 to FY25 for a total increase of nearly 30%.
Supervisor, Transmission Line 
Services

Article VII projects. These management employees also play a key role in overseeing contractors during storm restoration efforts. This position is in high 
demand across and US and will be required to develop the additional line workers and operators and successfully manage the increased capital plan which has 
nearly a 10% 
increase year over year from FY21 to FY25 for a total increase of approximately 35% for Transmission Line work.

Role from EIOP-13
Detailed Role Breakdown 
Justification of Role
Incremental Full-time Equivalents - Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel
Engineer
DPAM responsibilities for Distributed Generation currently include CESIR study activities, construction activities including submittal reviews, site visits, and 
witness tests, and general engineering support for distributed generation projects.  The trajectory to meet state clean energy goals is expected to increase to meet 
70% renewable generation by 2030 and 100% by 2040, with additional engineering needed to support expected growth in workload, along with building 
expertise to address unique new challenges driven by this unprecedented growth. Additionally, the Company aims to appropriately accelerate the EV market to 
enable an additional 800,000 ZEVs by 2025 from the approximated 47,000 that had been sold as of end of December 2019. Most notable the expanded Make-
Ready Program proposes an addition 24,312 Level 2 charging ports (~170 MW) as well as 600 DC Fast Chargers (~90 MW) to the Upstate New York system. 
Responsibilities for this work will include, technical reviews of each site to ensure the existing infrastructure is adequate and to design upgrades as applicable. 
Additionally, the Fleet Electrification Program proposes to support fleet customers in an initial electrification of a portion of their vehicles while also providing 
advisory services to develop a long-term plan for total fleet electrification. Responsibilities for this work will include identification of design of any required 
infrastructure upgrades in the near term, while also working closely with the design group to develop tabular estimates and designs to deliver to the customer as 
part of their individualized long-term fleet electrification road map. Inclusive to these programs, these roles will drive case specific managed charging schemes 
to minimize infrastructure upgrade costs, as well as promote the utilization of EV charging technologies which better position the Company to manage EV loads 
in higher penetration levels beyond 2025. 
Engineer
The Company is pursuing industry-recognized higher levels of Asset Management Maturity.  Internal teams have been determining current levels of maturity 
and identifying a roadmap to meet Company goals.  The roadmap is expected to require additional levels of engineering support to make more intelligent 
decisions on asset management.
SF6 Reduction Engineer
SF6 Reduction Engineer
National Grid is fully committed to its role in tackling climate change and has a critical position to help accelerate towards a cleaner future.  National Grid has 
committed to achieve the Company’s objective of net-zero carbon targets by 2050 and an 80% reduction in Sulfur Hexafluoride (SF6) emissions by 2030, a 
dedicated Engineer for the NY West Division is required to manage the SF6 reduction program to reduce greenhouse gas emissions.  SF6 has been, and is still, 
the primary insulation media in high voltage electrical equipment.  SF6 emissions levels have been significantly reduced in the past 10 years.  However, SF6 
handling and equipment maintenance efforts will need to be extremely aggressive to achieve even further reductions.  Engineering personnel dedicated 
exclusively to SF6 management are required to meet these reductions.  
Distribtuion Planning 
Engineer

d/b/a National Grid

Supervisor Regional Control
Supervisor Regional Control
Staffing increase to support evolving grid and market operations as a result of grid modernization and the inclusion of DER into wholesale and distribution 
markets. The staffing is in line with the forecasted needs associated with the actions needed to plan & implement the activities supporting NY’s Reforming the 
Energy Vision (REV) and the activities associated with the NYS Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act. The resource requirements are 
incremental and compliment the current resources as they will need an engineering skill set above and beyond the current operator positions. The additional 
coverage is needed to fully manage the changing market and grid operations as DER become more populous and markets and programs become more fully 
integrated.
Analyst
Lead Analyst 
A recommendation of the 2020 NMPC NERC TOP audit  was for National Grid NY to “improve the simulator technology used for restoration training to better 
reflect actual system response capabilities.”
The NY Operating Training Simulator (OTS) functions adequately for baseline operational scenarios, but cannot run more complex blackstart restoration 
scenarios. NMPC currently relies on training at the NYISO for these scenarios which provides limited opportunities to train operators on these events for the NY 
Operators. 
OTS is a critical component of the NERC GridEx restoration exercise that is conducted in conjunction with the NYISO and other Local Control Centers 
(LCC’s). Currently, only the NYISO simulator is used.  With the OTS Analyst, a local scenario can be created that can be tailored to the needs of the Niagara 
Mohawk Operators.
The current responsibility for OTS is with the CNI EMS team, who is also tasked with maintaining the State Estimator, Security Analysis and the other Network 
Applications. These Real Time Assessment tools are the operational priority, leaving OTS as an ancillary function. But OTS requires a lot of attention and 
adjustment, and very frequent interaction with ABB.
In order to make the improvements in the OTS necessary to follow the audit recommendation, it will be necessary to establish an OTS position in the NY 
Transmission Control Center which would be solely focused on the development and maintenance of the OTS. The resource would also be required to run the 
simulator during training scenarios to ensure delivery of quality training using the OTS.
Beyond keeping OTS up and running, the development of a blackstart restoration simulation will require a significant effort over a lengthy period of time. For 
comparison, the NYISO has a dedicated Simulator Engineer who also gets simulator support from one of the trainers and simulator support from their EMS 
staff. It took the resource months to configure the ABB OTS to adequately model the blackstart restoration simulation they use for the annual training.

Contract Specialist 
The Contract Strategy department estimates the ideal ratio of Contract Specialists to projects to be 25-40 projects per FTE. 
We are currently supporting only half of the projects with 3 FTE’s. We are requesting 3 additional FTE’s to be shared across op co’s to allow to support the 
additional projects due to the increase in the capital plan from FY22 to FY25. 
Contract Specialist 
The current frameworks we support can only be supported as extensions and rebids with the current staffing levels (4 FTE’s). In order to re evaluate the 
strategies and create new frameworks to support existing needs, we need an additional FTE. This FTE will be shared among the different op co’s.
The increase in workload in this rate case requires us to rethink for NY the T-Line & Substation frameworks and add a new framework for PTO. The current 
resources are not able to support this effort and a dedicated NY resource will be required to create and support those frameworks.

Specialist
Operational Technology 
Specialist
This position is required to support the increase of CAPEX and deployment of digital assets at National Grid substations. Digital substation technology is being 
deployed to drive long-term CAPEX and OPEX efficiency. The digital substation also facilitates new technology enhancements inherent in modern and future 
energy systems. This group is responsible to coordinate the various digital substation programs (such as IEC 61850 and On-Line Monitoring), engage internal 
and industry stakeholders, and track project & financial performance improvements. To take advantage of the digital technology efficiencies, the group will 
modify existing work methods and systems through extensive coordination with internal stakeholders such as operations, engineering, procurement/contract 
management, IT, and learning & development groups.

Eng Elec Plan & Design
A standards engineer position is needed to support significant growth in capital expenditure, especially within the Transmission Capex plan. The Transmission 
line portion of the overall transmission capex spend is increasing approximately 35% from FY21 to FY25. With this growth there is a need to better streamline 
processes to execute the work, review innovative technologies entering the market, stay current with industry standards, review new material vendors, update 
existing company standards and EOP’s, and much more. The standards engineer will also support design to value initiatives looking at alternative ways to 
design things to reduce costs, increase reliability, or improve safety. The initiatives created to date have generated significant efficiencies and more efficiencies 
can be realized with a dedicate resource supporting transmission line standards. 
Eng Elec Plan & Design
An engineer position is needed to support significant growth in capital expenditure, especially within the Transmission Capex plan. The Transmission line 
portion of the overall transmission capex spend is increasing approximately 35% from FY21 to FY25. (much of this increase is in asset condition driven 
projects). These projects require transmission line engineers and designers to execute. Four additional transmission line engineer FTE’s are needed to support 
this work. These resources will also support telecom work not included in the totals previously noted, as well as generator interconnection projects.

Lead Project Manager Gen

contract resource. As State and Company initiatives drive the interconnection of large scale renewable energy, complex applications at transmission and 
distribution levels are expected to continue to increase.  PD plays a large role with development of T Interconnect projects starting with the Feasibility studies.

existing resource loss risk and increase subject matter expertise so that the workload can be evenly distributed.

Role from EIOP-13
Detailed Role Breakdown 
Justification of Role
Incremental Full-time Equivalents - Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel
Specialist - Stakeholder 
Engagement
Snr Spec - Stkhldr Engmt
Since 2019, our project-to- specialist ratio has risen from 8-12 projects per specialist to an average 20-25 projects per FTE.  There is a need for an additional 
FTE to lower that ratio close the 8-12 project target in the coming fiscal years. The FY23 incremental increase reflects the need to add an additional FTE, an 
increase in staff by 20% (from 4 FTEs to 5 FTEs), to accommodate the approximate 20% increase in the capital work plan from FY 23-25.
There are several emerging factors which also contribute to the need for this staffing and an augmented public engagement team including:
- The increase in renewable energy projects which will require much more community engagement to explain interconnection needs and supplement or assist
engagement efforts of developers.
- The increased scrutiny of reliability projects by the public, particularly those that support existing natural  gas infrastructure. The public requires greater 
engagement and around the need, scope, and impact of these projects in light of recent climate policy goals.
- There is a greater demand for public engagement by community officials  on smaller-scale projects in general. These were not typically assigned an FTE, but
we will have to soon accommodate that need.
Lead Civil Engineer
Meet engineering demand and avoid heavy reliance on contractors for the increase in capital work to deliver thorough early-stage engineering.  This includes 
building a resilient system to allow future green energy interconnections, meet load growth, support customer interconnections and migrate our 
telecommunications resources to fiber.
Lead Electrical Engineer
Meet engineering demand and avoid heavy reliance on contractors for the increase in capital work to deliver thorough early-stage engineering.  This includes 
building a resilient system to allow future green energy interconnections, meet load growth, support customer interconnections and migrate our 
telecommunications resources to fiber.

Lead Eng Telecom 
Engineering
In addition to supporting the increased capital budget there are five specific communications projects totaling over $126M that telecom engineering is required 
to support.  They are the SCADA analog replacements program, migration of leased Teleprotection circuits to private networks, DMX upgrade for end of life 
equipment, upgrade of critical and key facilities communications and the upgrade of the Batavia fiber ring.  The capital plan breakdown over the FY21-FY25 
years are as follows: FY21 - ~$0.6M, FY22- ~$7M, FY23 – ~$19M, FY-24 – ~$45M, FY25 – ~$54M – Total FY21-25 - ~$126M.  The engineering support 
costs for telecom projects is typically in the range of 5% - 15%.  For the purposes of the rate case, 10% was used to estimate the internal telecom resource 
requirement.  Ten percent of the project cost amounts to ~$12.6M which then equates to 60,750 telecom engineering man-hours, or 29 FTE’s.  To support this 
telecom engineering FTE need between FY22-25, these FTE’s have been included in the rate case request with any additional support to be augmented with 
consultants.  The FTE’s will also provide ongoing support and upgrades for the life of the projects and new systems.

Role from EIOP-13
Detailed Role Breakdown 
Justification of Role
Incremental Full-time Equivalents - Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel
Designer B
•Designer positions needed to dedicate FTEs to development and maintenance of CADD processes and libraries for intelligent substation design. These 
processes and libraries will be used to promote better standardization for electric substations in NY.
• 3D i ntelligent substation design is a model-centric approach to design that consists of two components: 3D design and automated 
design:
- 3D design relates to 3-dimensional modeling of all substation components and utilization of LiDAR as needed. The use of 3D design with LiDAR reduces 
design errors caused by poor as-built conditions and allows quicker design as the model is changed with drawings created from it versus current manual CADD 
drafting of all drawings with many details repeated from drawing to drawing. The 3D models can also be utilized to make renderings and other permit visuals 
internally saving the expense of external contracts. 3D design would be utilized for all projects and is most beneficial with LiDAR for brownfield projects.
-I ntelligent design utilizes algorithms to automate wiring based upon elementary drawings and the 3D model of the substation, automate tallying materials & 
quantities of materials needed, it also automates clearance and other interference checks, and improves cost estimates, construction sequencing, engineering 
calculations, and other things. Intelligent design would be utilized on mostly greenfield and major rebuild projects. As the technology is developed there may 
be opportunities to apply to brownfield projects as well but on a more limited basis.
•  Using modern tools for CADD design with dedicated FTEs to develop and support helps with employee retention and staff development ultimately resulting in
better knowledge transfer and retention within National Grid.
•O   ther utilities (AEP, Duke Energy, PG&E, Hydro-Quebec, etc.) and engineering services contractors (TRC, Black & Veatch, Burns & McDonnell, Power 
Engineers, etc.) have been using intelligent design tools for years with efficiencies as stated or better.

Incremental Full-time Equivalents - Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel
Prgm Mgr Emergency 
Planning
FTEs dedicated to Emergency Planning in 2010 were essentially one (1) FTE which grew to two (2) upon the recommendations from the Moreland Commission 
after Superstorm Sandy.  
In 2017 Emergency Planning was restructured, adding a Director Role with responsibilities for NY, MA, and RI without adjusting the FTEs allocated to NY, 
which essentially decreased the dedicated FTE count to NY.  
Through turn-over, Emergency Planning has replaced the single FTE in NY with an employee who presently has military obligations that effectively reduce the 
Emergency Planning Electric FTE count of this single FTE to approximately .75 FTE.  
Lead Program Manager
Lead UAS Operations Program Manager - Provide the leadership and technical knowledge to arrange, organize, project manage, the new Unmanned Aircraft 
System (UAS) operations program within National Grid USA. The responsibilities will include leading and implementing UAS operations across the various 
functions within National Grid that can benefit from the use of UAS in the inspection, engineering, and construction of infrastructure.
Analyst
Analyst
Due to an observed increase in emergency cable alarms and deteriorating conditions on NMPC’s transmission cables serving the cities of Buffalo, Albany, and 
Syracuse and elsewhere, a more proactive inspection program is required to comply with our proposed internal Inspection and Maintenance (I&M) procedures 
for underground (UG) transmission.   The proposed I&M program provides for the ability to maximize the life of the assets and identify and address conditions 
that may lead to an asset failure.  The current age, condition, and leading performance indicators warrant a more frequent and proactive inspection program.

Justification of Role
Incremental Full-time Equivalents - Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel
Analyst
Incremental analyst positions are required to support business critical work processes including distribution design, work closure and data quality maintenance.  
These positions are necessary to address challenges that impact our ability to capitalize assets in a timely manner, efficiently progress work thru life cycles and 
provide consistent data management.   The need for these positions was identified by a cross functional business & IT TIGER team launched due to ongoing 
challenges.   This was supported by the executive stakeholders.   Responsibilities will include:
Task 1: Monitor, analyze and progress work requests (jobs).
Task 2: Monitor, investigate, reconcile/coordinating resolution of data quality issues in Smallworld GIS. 
Task 2 Benefits:  Needed to meet ADMS, Asset management and Grid Modernization data quality requirements, supports data management and governance 
aspirations & standards compliance (BMS)
Task 3: Provide Job Aids & Reference Guides, Training, Change & Enhancement services for EBU
Task 3 Benefits:  Process monitoring and continuous improvement by maintaining high level E2E workflows, development of job aids and reference guides, 
creation & maintenance of reference libraries, audit business use cases, coordinate appropriate updates for changes/enhancements with IT on behalf of all 
business stakeholders, communication with L&D and SME Trainers on changes/enhancements, identify and analyze inefficiencies, promote standardization and 
create and perform testing of process & system changes
Senior Analyst
