Grid Mod
Distribution Planning Engineer
Engineer
Kevin Stablewski
DPAM responsibilities for Distributed Generation currently include CESIR study activities, construction activities 
including submittal reviews, site visits, and witness tests, and general engineering support for distributed generation 
projects. The trajectory to meet state clean energy goals is expected to increase to meet 70% renewable generation by 2030 
and 100% by 2040, with additional engineering needed to support expected growth in workload, along with building 
expertise to address unique new challenges driven by this unprecedented growth. Additionally, the Company aims to 
appropriately accelerate the EV market to enable an additional 800,000 ZEVs by 2025 from the approximated 47,000 that 
had been sold as of end of December 2019. Most notable the expanded Make-ready Program proposes an addition 24,312 
Level 2 charging ports (~170 MW) as well as 600 DC Fast Chargers (~90 MW) to the Upstate New York system. 
Responsibilities for this work will include, technical reviews of each site to ensure the existing infrastructure is adequate 
and to design upgrades as applicable. Additionally, the Fleet Electrification Program proposes to support fleet customers in 
an initial electrification of a portion of their vehicles while also providing advisory services to develop a long-term plan for 
total fleet electrification. Responsibilities for this work will include identification of design of any required infrastructure 
upgrades in the near term, while also working closely with the design group to develop tabular estimates and designs to 
deliver to the customer as part of their individualized long-term fleet electrification road map. Inclusive to these programs, 
these roles will drive case specific managed charging schemes to minimize infrastructure upgrade costs, as well as promote 
the utilization of EV charging technologies which better position the Company to manage EV loads in higher penetration 
levels beyond 2025.

NY
Grid Mod
SF6 Reduction Engineer
SF6 Reduction Engineer
Eileen Duarte
National Grid is fully committed to its role in tackling climate change and has a critical position to help accelerate towards 
a cleaner future. National Grid has committed to achieve the Company’s objective of net-zero carbon targets by 2050 and 
an 80% reduction in Sulfur Hexafluoride (SF6) emissions by 2030, a dedicated Engineer for the NY West Division is 
required to manage the SF6 reduction program to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. SF6 has been, and is still, the primary 
insulation media in high voltage electrical equipment. SF6 emissions levels have been significantly reduced in the past 10 
years. However, SF6 handling and equipment maintenance efforts will need to be extremely aggressive to achieve even 
further reductions. Engineering personnel dedicated exclusively to SF6 management are required to meet these reductions.
3

Grid Mod
Analyst
TCC Lead Analyst
Joe Farella
A recommendation of the 2020 NMPC NERC TOP audit was for National Grid NY to “improve the simulator technology 
used for restoration training to better reflect actual system response capabilities.” The NY Operating Training Simulator 
(OTS) functions adequately for baseline operational scenarios, but cannot run more complex black start restoration 
scenarios. NMPC currently relies on training at the NYISO for these scenarios which provides limited opportunities to train 
operators on these events for the NY Operators. OTS is a critical component of the NERC GridEx restoration exercise that 
is conducted in conjunction with the NYISO and other Local Control Centers (LCC’s). Currently, only the NYISO 
simulator is used. With the OTS Analyst, a local scenario can be created that can be tailored to the needs of the Niagara 
Mohawk Operators. The current responsibility for OTS is with the CNI EMS team, who is also tasked with maintaining the 
State Estimator, Security Analysis and the other Network Applications. These Real Time Assessment tools are the 
operational priority, leaving OTS as an ancillary function. But OTS requires a lot of attention and adjustment, and very 
frequent interaction with ABB. In order to make the improvements in the OTS necessary to follow the audit 
recommendation, it will be necessary to establish an OTS position in the NY Transmission Control Center which would be 
solely focused on the development and maintenance of the OTS. The resource would also be required to run the simulator 
during training scenarios to ensure delivery of quality training using the OTS. Beyond keeping OTS up and running, the 
development of a black start restoration simulation will require a significant effort over a lengthy period of time. For 
comparison, the NYISO has a dedicated Simulator Engineer who also gets simulator support from one of the trainers and 
simulator support from their EMS staff. It took the resource months to configure the ABB OTS to adequately model the 
black start restoration simulation they use for the annual training.
1
Please See DPS-660 Supp (Analyst)

Capex Growth Support
Specialist - Stakeholder Engagement
Snr Spec - Stkhldr Engmt
Marc Lucas
Since 2019, our project-to- specialist ratio has risen from 8-12 projects per specialist to an average 20-25 projects per FTE. 
There is a need for an additional FTE to lower that ratio close the 8-12 project target in the coming fiscal years. The FY23 
incremental increase reflects the need to add an additional FTE, an increase in staff by 20% (from 4 FTEs to 5 FTEs), to 
accommodate the approximate 27% increase in the capital work plan from FY 23-25. There are several emerging factors 
which also contribute to the need for this staffing and an augmented public engagement team including: - The increase in 
renewable energy projects which will require much more community engagement to explain interconnection needs and 
supplement or assist engagement efforts of developers. - The increased scrutiny of reliability projects by the public, 
particularly those that support existing natural gas infrastructure. The public requires greater engagement and around the 
need, scope, and impact of these projects in light of recent climate policy goals. - There is a greater demand for public 
engagement by community officials on smaller-scale projects in general. These were not typically assigned an FTE, but we 
will have to soon accommodate that need.

Please see DPS-660 Supp (Engineer /

NY
Capex Growth Support
Telecom Engineer
Lead Eng Telecom Engineering
Len Swanson 
In addition to supporting the increased capital budget there are five specific communications projects totaling over $126M 
that telecom engineering is required to support. They are the SCADA analog replacements program, migration of leased 
Teleprotection circuits to private networks, DMX upgrade for end of life equipment, upgrade of critical and key facilities 
communications and the upgrade of the Batavia fiber ring. The capital plan breakdown over the FY21-FY25 years are as 
follows: FY21 - ~$0.6M, FY22- ~$7M, FY23 – ~$19M, FY-24 – ~$45M, FY25 – ~$54M – Total FY21-25 - ~$126M. The 
engineering support costs for telecom projects is typically in the range of 5% - 15%. For the purposes of the rate case, 10% 
was used to estimate the internal telecom resource requirement. Ten percent of the project cost amounts to ~$12.6M which 
then equates to 60,750 telecom engineering man-hours, or 29 FTE’s. To support this telecom engineering FTE need 
between FY22-25, these FTE’s have been included in the rate case request with any additional support to be augmented 
with consultants. The FTE’s will also provide ongoing support and upgrades for the life of the projects and new systems.

Please see DPS-660 Supp (Substation 
Engineer)
NY
Capex Growth Support
Substation Designer
Designer B
Mark Larrabee
•Designer positions needed to dedicate FTEs to development and maintenance of CADD processes and libraries for 
intelligent substation design. These processes and libraries will be used to promote better standardization for electric 
substations in NY. • 3D intelligent substation design is a model-centric approach to design that consists of two components: 
3D design and automated design: - 3D design relates to 3-dimensional modeling of all substation components and 
utilization of LiDAR as needed. The use of 3D design with LiDAR reduces design errors caused by poor as-built conditions 
and allows quicker design as the model is changed with drawings created from it versus current manual CADD drafting of 
all drawings with many details repeated from drawing to drawing. The 3D models can also be utilized to make renderings 
and other permit visuals internally saving the expense of external contracts. 3D design would be utilized for all projects 
and is most beneficial with LiDAR for brownfield projects. -I intelligent design utilizes algorithms to automate wiring 
based upon elementary drawings and the 3D model of the substation, automate tallying materials & quantities of materials 
needed, it also automates clearance and other interference checks, and improves cost estimates, construction sequencing, 
engineering calculations, and other things. Intelligent design would be utilized on mostly greenfield and major rebuild 
projects. As the technology is developed there may be opportunities to apply to brownfield projects as well but on a more 
limited basis. • Using modern tools for CADD design with dedicated FTEs to develop and support helps with employee 
retention and staff development ultimately resulting in better knowledge transfer and retention within National Grid. 
•Othere utilities (AEP, Duke Energy, PG&E, Hydro-Quebec, etc.) and engineering services contractors (TRC, Black & 
Veatch, Burns & McDonnell, Power Engineers, etc.) have been using intelligent design tools for years.
2
2

Request: 
Note: In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be 
construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in 
original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
For each incremental position/role identified in Exhibit__(EIOP-13), provide the following 
information: 
1.
For the incremental labor identified in the Direct Testimony of the Energy Infrastructure
and Operations Panel, pgs. 120 –123, and Exhibit__(EIOP-13), provide a detailed
explanation and discussion on how the Company plans to fill the large amount of
incremental positions.
2.
In recent conversations with electric utilities in New York, it seems to be difficult to find
and hire new employees.  Explain why or why not the Company believes it can obtain the
levels of incremental positions identified in the filing?
Response:  
1-2.  
The Company’s staffing strategy to hire the incremental resources requested in the filing
includes the following: 

Talent Acquisition Team – National Grid’s Talent Acquisition Team has proven it is
able to deploy capacity, capability, and channels to identify, attract, and hire
incremental resources.  In Niagara Mohawk’s service territory, there is a great deal of
interest in careers in the utility industry, which has resulted in there being a sufficient
population of candidates to meet its hiring needs to date.  Talent Acquisition’s
marketing strategy is to post externally to a broad array of job boards as outlined in
Attachment 1. The Company’s recruiters are organized into areas of specialization
with responsibility for ongoing development of market contacts.  Recruiters are
Exhibit __ (EIOP-6R) 
Page 32 of 108


--- Page 138 ---
Form 103 
provided with tools such as LinkedIn recruiter and Talent Neuron to identify passive 
candidates proactively.  In addition, when required for more challenging or hard to 
fill roles, the Company may employ third party search firms to assist with finding 
experienced candidates.  Attachment 1 provides an overview of the work performed 
by the Talent Acquisition Team.

Future Development Efforts - To build a pipeline for future workers and provide them
with the skills and tools they will need, the Company has established development
programs and partnered with vocational schools, community colleges, and veterans’
associations.  For example, the Campus to Careers team within Talent Acquisition is
responsible for the recruitment, selection, onboarding and program management for
three early talent programs:  Intern Program, Graduate Development Program, and
Leadership Development Program.  These programs are described in more detail in
Attachment 1.
Niagara Mohawk has partnered with various colleges throughout its service territory on 
programs designed to prepare students for entry-level positions in the electrical and/or 
gas utility industry.  Examples of where these programs have been held include: 
o
Overhead Electric Lineworker Certificate (Erie CC & Hudson Valley CC)
o
Line Mechanic Utility Worker Certificate (Onondaga CC)
o
AOS in Electric (Onondaga CC)
o
Troops to Energy Jobs – Natural Gas Boot Camp (Jefferson CC)
o
MS in Business of Energy (Clarkson University)
In addition, the Company, along with other utilities across the country in partnership with 
the Center for Energy Workforce Development, created the Troops to Energy Jobs (T2E) 
initiative to assist military veterans in finding careers in energy. 
Niagara Mohawk believes that these efforts will provide the support needed to hire the incremental 
resources requested in the filing.

our hiring needs. Please note any approach or timeline can and will be impacted by recruiting volumes and 
market conditions or candidate availability.  In the event capacity is challenged, timelines can become 
longer.  National Grid is committed to filling all incremental positions through our advertised channels (slide 
3 – Where Jobs Are Posted ) and continues to focus on our future talent (slide 4 – Future Talent 
Opportunities).

selection, onboarding & program management for 3 early talent programs: Intern Program, 
Graduate Development Program, Leadership Development Program. Through targeted 
university outreach and relationships, Campus to Careers seeks to attract and develop high 
potential student talent to build a pipeline for current and future business needs.

National Grid

•

o

o

o
Candidate Status – if candidate selected, monitor progress to hire
•
Partnering with a third party consultant on sourcing strategy and performance
•
Quarterly review of job posting sites and performance
•
Annual evaluation of current site performance and new sites

National Grid 
National Grid provides opportunities to students in accredited college or university 
undergraduate and graduate programs to gain valuable practical experiences related to 
their fields of study through internships and co-ops. Interns/Co-ops receive practical 
experience in the energy industry, while we get to know potential hires who could be a 
good long-term fit within our company. Intern categories:
•
Summer interns
•
Off-cycle interns: hired to work during the school year (fall/spring semester).
•
Co-op assignments: for students enrolled in an accredited degree and will typically
work on a full-time basis. Co-op lengths and requirements will vary depending on the
school’s Co-op policy. Most co-op assignments are 3-6 months in length
Gridtern (Intern) Programs

National Grid 
Graduate Development Program Overview
National Grid’s Graduate Development Program (GDP) is the 
company’s flagship pipeline program, targeting recent college graduates 
joining the company in entry-level full time positions. 
The GDP is designed to prepare early career professionals for future 
leadership roles within the organization, aims to support Graduates
in developing as leaders, guide their career, and increase 
their business talents and skills.

14
National Grid 
Graduate Development Program Components
GDP Orientation
Graduates start their careers at National Grid with an 8-week 
Orientation. Graduates will spend time learning the core functions of the 
business, networking with our Executive team, and traveling to our 
different jurisdictional areas. By the end of Orientation, Graduates  have 
an in depth understanding of who National Grid is as a company and 
how we do business. They have also formed a network with their fellow 
Graduates as well as many business contacts, which will only help them 
in their full time roles. 
Quarterly Development Sessions
Throughout the first year, the Graduates will attend a series of strategic 
training sessions aligned to National Grid’s leadership qualities and core 
values and technical training courses.
Capstone Project
This project aims to accelerate the Graduates’ understanding of the 
whole company, allows them to utilize the networks built during 
Orientation and their first year of service, and strengthens the skills 
needed for working in business teams. The Capstone Project is 
sponsored by Executives who provide the topic area and project outline.

d/b/a National Grid

15
National Grid 
Leadership Development Program Overview
National Grid’s Leadership Development (LDP) Program is designed to recruit and develop 
high-potential leaders with diverse backgrounds who can lead the organization through the 
challenges of the coming years. LDP participants are recent MBA graduates with 3-5 years 
prior full time experience. 
LDP members complete rotations in different areas of the company and ‘graduate’ 
the program well-equipped to enter permanent leadership roles.
The program accelerates members’ professional development through expanding internal 
networks, addressing key challenges facing the company, and encouraging strategic 
thinking. Members leverage their rotation experiences and leadership abilities to create 
lasting impact.
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation 
d/b/a National Grid

--- Page 161 ---
16
National Grid 
Leadership Development Program Components
Induction
LDP participants start with National Grid in mid-July and take part in a 4-week induction that includes an in-
depth overview of how the business operates. The participants will travel to various National Grid locations 
and have opportunities to build relationships with leaders across the organization. Induction also includes 
leadership development opportunities for the participants, including a comprehensive case study project. 
Upon conclusion of induction, the LDP participants report to their first rotation placement. 
Rotations
Incoming LDP participants begin their first rotation in August following Induction. This first rotation lasts 8 
months. Participants on program typically start new rotations in April and October and the rotations last 6 
months each. Rotations are meant to be in high impact areas of the organization and allow participants to 
build their core competencies and leadership ability while gaining a cross-functional perspective.
Development
During their time in the program, LDP participants contribute to the growth and success of the program. The 
LDP participants meet regularly to work on projects together related to the program and connect with each 
other regarding their experiences. LDP participants take a significant role in the recruitment process for the 
incoming cohort. Additionally, they plan and attend various leadership development opportunities, including 
meetings with National Grid executives. 
Post Rotation Placement
LDP participants are encouraged to begin seeking permanent placement throughout either their third or fourth 
rotation. The goal is that LDP participants move into positions that are involved in driving change across the 
organization. These roles should be opportunities where they can leverage their external experience, 
education and the knowledge acquired during rotations into a position in a transformational manner (i.e. 
manager of others, a product owner, project manager, program manager).
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation 
d/b/a National Grid

National Grid Initiatives

entry level roles.

Capital District in affiliation with SUNY Morrisville and Hudson Valley Community College provides hands on 
training to program participants.  Due to COVID, this program is anticipated to resume in January 2021.
Energy Production Distribution & Safety Specialization:  SUNY Buffalo offers an introductory course for 
students considering a career in the utility industry
Grid for Good has an aspiration to lead the way to create and accelerate the supply of talent coming into the 
utility industry to meet the demand and close the gap across the full industry. Partnering with colleagues in 
Grid for Good to develop methods on how National Grid is developing a pipeline that is filled by tapping into 
unusual places within our communities. 
Energy Industry Fundamentals curriculum for entry-level jobs in the utility industry.
• SUNY Farmingdale and CUNY Kingsborough Community College delivers on-line instruction
• Center for Energy Workforce Development (CEWD) provides content to schools sponsored by CEWD
member companies in the National Energy Educator Network (NEEN).

National Grid 
Graduate Development Program Overview
National Grid’s Graduate Development Program (GDP) is the 
company’s flagship pipeline program, targeting recent college graduates 
joining the company in entry-level full time positions. 
The GDP is designed to prepare early career professionals for future 
leadership roles within the organization, aims to support Graduates
in developing as leaders, guide their career, and increase 
their business talents and skills.

14
National Grid 
Graduate Development Program Components
GDP Orientation
Graduates start their careers at National Grid with an 8-week 
Orientation. Graduates will spend time learning the core functions of the 
business, networking with our Executive team, and traveling to our 
different jurisdictional areas. By the end of Orientation, Graduates  have 
an in depth understanding of who National Grid is as a company and 
how we do business. They have also formed a network with their fellow 
Graduates as well as many business contacts, which will only help them 
in their full time roles. 
Quarterly Development Sessions
Throughout the first year, the Graduates will attend a series of strategic 
training sessions aligned to National Grid’s leadership qualities and core 
values and technical training courses.
Capstone Project
This project aims to accelerate the Graduates’ understanding of the 
whole company, allows them to utilize the networks built during 
Orientation and their first year of service, and strengthens the skills 
needed for working in business teams. The Capstone Project is 
sponsored by Executives who provide the topic area and project outline.

National Grid 
Leadership Development Program Overview
National Grid’s Leadership Development (LDP) Program is designed to recruit and develop 
high-potential leaders with diverse backgrounds who can lead the organization through the 
challenges of the coming years. LDP participants are recent MBA graduates with 3-5 years 
prior full time experience. 
LDP members complete rotations in different areas of the company and ‘graduate’ 
the program well-equipped to enter permanent leadership roles.
The program accelerates members’ professional development through expanding internal 
networks, addressing key challenges facing the company, and encouraging strategic 
thinking. Members leverage their rotation experiences and leadership abilities to create 
lasting impact.
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation 
d/b/a National Grid

16
National Grid 
Leadership Development Program Components
Induction
LDP participants start with National Grid in mid-July and take part in a 4-week induction that includes an in-
depth overview of how the business operates. The participants will travel to various National Grid locations 
and have opportunities to build relationships with leaders across the organization. Induction also includes 
leadership development opportunities for the participants, including a comprehensive case study project. 
Upon conclusion of induction, the LDP participants report to their first rotation placement. 
Rotations
Incoming LDP participants begin their first rotation in August following Induction. This first rotation lasts 8 
months. Participants on program typically start new rotations in April and October and the rotations last 6 
months each. Rotations are meant to be in high impact areas of the organization and allow participants to 
build their core competencies and leadership ability while gaining a cross-functional perspective.
Development
During their time in the program, LDP participants contribute to the growth and success of the program. The 
LDP participants meet regularly to work on projects together related to the program and connect with each 
other regarding their experiences. LDP participants take a significant role in the recruitment process for the 
incoming cohort. Additionally, they plan and attend various leadership development opportunities, including 
meetings with National Grid executives. 
Post Rotation Placement
LDP participants are encouraged to begin seeking permanent placement throughout either their third or fourth 
rotation. The goal is that LDP participants move into positions that are involved in driving change across the 
organization. These roles should be opportunities where they can leverage their external experience, 
education and the knowledge acquired during rotations into a position in a transformational manner (i.e. 
manager of others, a product owner, project manager, program manager).

d/b/a National Grid

National Grid Initiatives
National Grid partners with the following to build talent pipeline to ensure qualified hires for 
entry level roles.
Customer Meter Service Technician Certificate: Educational Opportunity Center (EOC) in Syracuse and the 
Capital District in affiliation with SUNY Morrisville and Hudson Valley Community College provides hands on 
training to program participants.  Due to COVID, this program is anticipated to resume in January 2021.
Energy Production Distribution & Safety Specialization:  SUNY Buffalo offers an introductory course for 
students considering a career in the utility industry
Grid for Good: has an aspiration to lead the way to create and accelerate the supply of talent coming into the 
utility industry to meet the demand and close the gap across the full industry. Partnering with colleagues in 
Grid for Good to develop methods on how National Grid is developing a pipeline that is filled by tapping into 
unusual places within our communities. 
Electrical Construction and Maintenance Program (ECM):  Hudson Valley Community College offers a 
degreed program to prepare students for a career in the electrical industry.
Overhead Electric Line Worker Certificate:  Hudson Valley Community College offers a certificate program to 
prepare students for a career in the electric utility industry.
Energy Industry Fundamentals: curriculum for entry-level jobs in the utility industry.
• SUNY Farmingdale and CUNY Kingsborough Community College delivers on-line instruction
• Center for Energy Workforce Development (CEWD) provides content to schools sponsored by CEWD 
member companies in the National Energy Educator Network (NEEN).

Response:  
1.
Please see Attachment 1.  Attachment 1 is confidential and will be submitted in
accordance with the Protective Order issued in these proceedings.
2.
The Company has not yet developed the requested planned circuit drawings.  Please see
the project description in the Company’s response to Question 6, below.
3. Although some outages may have occurred on this circuit affecting customers, the
transmission circuit generally has very good reliability; however, the justification for
resiliency projects is not based on actual outages or events.  The justification for projects
under this spending rationale is proactive rather than reactive and is based on
consideration of low frequency – high impact events.  For example, a substation built in a
100-year floodplain may have never experienced any flooding, but, if it were to become
flooded, it would create a large-scale outage of several thousand customers.  To identify
and prioritize system risks to low frequency-high impact events, the Company performed
a deterministic review of the number of customers that could be affected by a failure of
transmission element(s) that would result in an outage of the Clay – Dewitt circuit.
The Company does have a Worst Performing Circuit program to address historic 
transmission performance issues that adversely impact reliability.    
4.
Please see the Company’s response to Question 3, above.
5.
This project is still in the planning stages and an approved sanction paper is not yet
available.
6.
This project proposes to move the 6.7 mile long Duguid station tap off the 16 mile long
Clay – Dewitt #5 line and connect it directly to the Dewitt station bus via a new breaker
position, creating a new line.
An outage of the Clay – Dewitt #5 line results in a sustained outage to 9,500 customers
and a momentary outage to 1,500 customers.  By splitting the Clay – Dewitt line into two
lines, the overhead line exposure to a fault is reduced for customers served from both
circuits.  The accuracy of distance to fault calculations will also be improved, which will
result in faster identification of the fault location.

switches 194 and 193.  This is a total of four breakers to sectionalize DeWitt - Tilden #19
into three lines.
Loss of the Dewitt – Tilden #19 circuit interrupts 6,400 customers at Southwood Station
and 5,600 customers at Pebble Hill Station.  The addition of breakers to this circuit will
separate this line into three line sections.  The sustained outages of the 12,000 customers
at Pebble Hill and Southwood stations will be eliminated.  With the possible location of

the project description in the Company’s response to Question 6, below.
3.
Although some outages may have occurred on this circuit affecting customers, the
transmission circuit generally has very good reliability; however, the justification for
resiliency projects is not based on actual outages or events.  The justification for projects
under this spending rationale is proactive rather than reactive and is based on
consideration of low frequency – high impact events.  For example, a substation built in a
100-year floodplain may have never experienced any flooding, but, if it were to become
flooded, it would create a large-scale outage of several thousand customers.  To identify
and prioritize system risks to low frequency-high impact events, the Company performed
a deterministic review of the number of customers that could be affected by a failure of a
transmission element(s) that would result in an outage of the “customer station” - Albany

The project is in the planning stages and an approved sanction paper is not yet available.
6.
This project installs an Automatic Line Sectionalizing (“ALS”) scheme at New Krumkill
utilizing existing 115kV motor-operated disconnects MOD 788 and MOD 888.  The ALS
scheme consists primarily of relay and control equipment.
Loss of the circuit between Albany and the customer transmission station interrupts the
Mckownville and New Krumkill stations. The Mckownville station has an ALS scheme
that will restore customers at that station to service once the fault has cleared; however,
the 6,800 customers at New Krumkill may not be restored depending on the location of
the fault.  Following a line outage, the ALS will isolate the faulted section of the line and
restore customers.  With the possible location of the fault narrowed down to a smaller
section of the line, it will be possible to identify and rectify the problem area more
quickly.
An itemized cost of equipment is not available at this stage in the project’s development.
Projects in the early stage of development typically do not have more detailed project
estimate breakdowns.  A more detailed cost breakdown will be developed as the project
progresses through the process.

1.

2.
The Company has not yet developed the requested planned circuit drawings.  Please see
the project description in the Company’s response to Question 6, below.
3.
Although some outages may have occurred on this circuit affecting customers, the
transmission circuit generally has very good reliability; however, the justification for
resiliency projects is not based on actual outages or events.  The justification for projects
under this spending rationale is proactive rather than reactive and is based on
consideration of low frequency – high impact events.  For example, a substation built in a
100-year floodplain may have never experienced any flooding, but, if it were to become
flooded, it would create a large-scale outage of several thousand customers.  To identify
and prioritize system risks to low frequency-high impact events, the Company performed
a deterministic review of the number of customers that could be affected by a failure of a
transmission element(s) that would result in an outage of the North Troy - Hoosick
circuit.
The Company does have a Worst Performing Circuit program to address historic 
transmission performance issues that adversely impact reliability.   
4.
Please see the Company’s response to Question 3, above.
5.
The project is in the planning stages and an approved sanction paper is not yet available.
6.
This project adds remote control capability to the existing 115kV switches (511, 522) at
the Boyntonville tap.
An outage of the North Troy – Hoosick circuit interrupts 5,300 customers at
Boyntonville.  The remoteness of this station and difficulty accessing the transmission
line results in delays identifying the fault locations and thus long restoration times.
Following a line outage, the switches can be used to isolate the faulted section of the line
and restore customers.  With the possible location of the fault narrowed down to a smaller
section of the lines, it will be possible to identify and rectify the problem area more
quickly.
An itemized cost of equipment is not available at this stage in the project’s development.

progresses through the process.

6.
This project constructs a new 230/115kV station at Riverview Rd where the Rotterdam –
Eastover 230kV line is parallel to the Rotterdam – Maplewood 115kV lines and
reconfigures some of the existing 115kV lines.
Eight lines in series form one path from Rotterdam to Maplewood.  An outage of the first
line out of Rotterdam and the first line out of Maplewood will result in an interruption to
42,000 customers.  Other combinations of line outages would also interrupt a significant
number of customers.  By creating the new station at Riverview Road, a third source will
be added into this load pocket and the number of customers interrupted for an outage of
any two lines would be reduced from up to 42,000 to no more than 16,700.
An itemized cost of equipment is not available at this stage in the project’s development.
Projects in the early stage of development typically do not have more detailed project
estimate breakdowns.  A more detailed cost breakdown will be developed as the project
progresses through the process.

the event the Company’s actual costs are substantially lower than projected.  The

Case Nos. 20-E-0380 and 20-G-0381 
Electric and Gas Rates 
Request for Information 
FROM: 
DPS Staff, Anthony DiGiacomo 
TO: 
National Grid, Revenue Requirements & Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
Panels 
SUBJECT:  
Storm Fund 
Request: 
Note:
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall 
be construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet 
models in original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
Pages 116-118 of the Electric Infrastructure & Operations Panel discusses the Company’s 
proposal to implement a two-way reconciliation mechanism for minor storm costs. 
1.
What specific rate allowance does NMPC propose to reconcile the minor storm costs
around?
2.
For calendar years 2010 through 2019, provide the supporting documentation behind
minor storm costs broken out by each storm event.  This should include, but not be
limited to, expenses broken down by incremental and base costs, by the different cost
components, and number of customers interrupted.
3.
Provide the Company’s rationale and supporting data behind the $10 million deadband.
4.
Provide all accounting and training procedures in place to prevent NMPC employees
from inaccurately charging minor storm work time as non-storm work time or vice versa.
Response:  
1.
See Attachment 1 for the detail by cost element of Historic Test Year (HTY) minor storm
costs inflated to the Rate Year.  The total Rate Year allowance for minor storm expense
included in the Company’s filing is $32.683 million as presented on Attachment 1, and is
the amount that the Company proposes to reconcile around, using a $10 million dead
band as described in the testimony of the Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel.
This $32.683 million minor storm allowance is predicated on adoption of the Company’s
Exhibit __ (EIOP-6R) 
Page 102 of 108


--- Page 208 ---
Form 103 
proposal to align the definition of a deferrable major storm with the definition used for 
reliability purposes, as discussed in the Company’s response to DPS-524.  If the major 
storm definition change is not adopted, the minor storm expense allowance should 
increase to $58.815 million to include the costs associated with storms that are major 
based on the reliability definition but not deferrable based on the current definition of a 
deferrable major storm.  The analysis for this response resulted in a recategorization of 
$4.011 million in costs associated with the 12/29/2019 major storm event that were 
included in minor storm costs in the Company’s initial filing and moved to major 
deferrable and major non-deferrable storm costs.  The work paper supporting the HTY 
adjustments to remove deferrable major storm costs provided in Exhibit___(RRP-3), 
Schedules 2 will be updated accordingly in the Company’s correction and updates filing. 
2.
The Company does not maintain or have readily available statistics on customer outages
that are linked to minor storms.  Minor storms are not defined by events, but, rather are
any weather-caused outages not meeting the criteria of a major storm event.  Minor storm
costs are recorded on the Company’s general ledger to one work order per division per
month and not associated with any specific event.  Attachment 2 presents minor storm
costs incurred by the Company broken down by base and incremental, and by cost
element.  As discussed with Staff, in Case 17-E-0238 the Company provided cost
information from calendar year 2013 and forward because of challenges obtaining pre-
2013 information due to moving from a legacy general ledger system to the SAP system.
If Staff advises the Company that data prior to 2013 is needed for this response, the
Company will determine what information can be provided and the estimated timeframe
for doing so.

Date of Response: 
December 14, 2020 
Name of Respondent: 
Aferdita Bardhi and Anthony DiGiacomo 
Subject: 
Major Storms 
Question: 
1. On page 130 of the SEIOP testimony, Staff recommends adjusting the major storm deferral
threshold from $0.400 million to $0.750 million.  Please indicate whether Staff’s
recommended threshold of $0.750 million would be based on (i) the total incremental costs
for all eight regions for a single storm event, or (ii) the incremental costs for one region
resulting from a single storm event.
2. On page 134 of the SEIOP testimony, Staff recommends a reduction of $1.0 million to the
Company’s proposed major storm expense reserve allowance, from $31.0 million to $30.0
million, to reflect its proposed $0.750 million deferral threshold.  In Exhibit__(SRRP-1) to
the Staff Revenue Requirement Panel, however, Staff presents an adjustment of $1.219
million to the Company’s proposed $31.0 million (see Schedule 2, Page 2 of 9, “Storm Fund”
line item).  Please confirm the correct recommended adjustment amount to the major storm
expense reserve allowance.
Response: 
1. Staff’s recommendation for the $0.750 million major storm threshold should be applied to
the total incremental costs for the region(s) that meet the major storm definition for a single
storm event.  Incremental restoration costs in regions which do not meet the major storm
definition should not be applied to the major storm threshold.
2. Exhibit___(SRRP-1), Schedule 2, should have reflected an adjustment to the major storm
expense reserve allowance in the amount of $1.0 million.
